<details>
<summary>138 lines</summary>

````
1. # Taskbar Alignment Tool
2. 
3. A Windows 11 system-tray app that dynamically adjusts taskbar settings based on your primary display's effective resolution. When you dock/undock a laptop or change monitors, the taskbar automatically adapts.
4. 
5. ## Features
6. 
7. - **Resolution profiles** — Define breakpoints that control taskbar alignment, button combining, and taskbar size
8. - **System tray** — Runs silently in the notification area with a right-click context menu
9. - **JSON config** — Edit `config.json` to customize profiles, thresholds, and poll interval
10. - **Run at Startup** — Toggle auto-start from the tray menu (MSIX StartupTask or registry)
11. - **No admin required** — All settings are under `HKCU`
12. 
13. ## Default Profiles
14. 
15. | Profile | Min Width | Alignment | Combine Buttons | Taskbar Size |
16. |---------|-----------|-----------|-----------------|--------------|
17. | Compact | 0 px | Left | Never | Small |
18. | Standard | 1920 px | Left | When Full | Default |
19. | Ultrawide | 2560 px | Center | Always | Default |
20. 
21. The highest `minWidth` that fits your current effective resolution wins.
22. 
23. ## Requirements
24. 
25. - **Windows 11** (the `TaskbarAl`, `TaskbarGlomLevel`, and `TaskbarSi` registry keys are Windows 11 specific)
26. - [.NET 8 SDK](https://dotnet.microsoft.com/download/dotnet/8.0) (to build)
27. 
28. ## Build
29. 
30. ```powershell
31. dotnet build
32. ```
33. 
34. ## Run
35. 
36. ```powershell
37. dotnet run --project TaskbarAlignmentTool
38. ```
39. 
40. The app starts in the system tray (notification area). Right-click the tray icon for options.
41. 
42. ## Publish (self-contained executable)
43. 
44. ```powershell
45. dotnet publish TaskbarAlignmentTool -c Release -r win-x64 --self-contained -o publish
46. ```
47. 
48. This produces a standalone `publish\TaskbarAlignmentTool.exe` that doesn't require .NET to be installed.
49. 
50. ## Configuration
51. 
52. On first run, a default `config.json` is created at:
53. 
54. ```
55. %LOCALAPPDATA%\TaskbarAlignmentTool\config.json
56. ```
57. 
58. You can also open it from the tray menu → **Open Config**.
59. 
60. ### Example config
61. 
62. ```json
63. {
64.   "refreshIntervalMs": 5000,
65.   "profiles": [
66.     {
67.       "name": "Compact",
68.       "minWidth": 0,
69.       "alignment": "Left",
70.       "combineButtons": "Never",
71.       "taskbarSize": "Small"
72.     },
73.     {
74.       "name": "Standard",
75.       "minWidth": 1920,
76.       "alignment": "Left",
77.       "combineButtons": "WhenFull",
78.       "taskbarSize": "Default"
79.     },
80.     {
81.       "name": "Ultrawide",
82.       "minWidth": 2560,
83.       "alignment": "Center",
84.       "combineButtons": "Always",
85.       "taskbarSize": "Default"
86.     }
87.   ]
88. }
89. ```
90. 
91. ### Config options
92. 
93. | Field | Values |
94. |-------|--------|
95. | `refreshIntervalMs` | Poll interval in milliseconds (minimum 500) |
96. | `alignment` | `Left`, `Center` |
97. | `combineButtons` | `Always`, `WhenFull`, `Never` |
98. | `taskbarSize` | `Small`, `Default`, `Large` |
99. 
100. After editing, use tray menu → **Reload Config** to apply changes immediately.
101. 
102. ## Registry Keys
103. 
104. The app writes to `HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\Advanced`:
105. 
106. | Key | Values | Effect |
107. |-----|--------|--------|
108. | `TaskbarAl` | 0 = Left, 1 = Center | Taskbar alignment |
109. | `TaskbarGlomLevel` | 0 = Always, 1 = When Full, 2 = Never | Combine taskbar buttons |
110. | `TaskbarSi` | 0 = Small, 1 = Default, 2 = Large | Taskbar size |
111. 
112. ## Project Structure
113. 
114. ```
115. TaskbarAlignmentTool/
116. ├── Program.cs                  # Entry point
117. ├── TrayApplicationContext.cs   # System tray icon and context menu
118. ├── DisplayMonitor.cs           # Polls primary display effective resolution
119. ├── TaskbarAligner.cs           # Writes registry keys, notifies Explorer
120. ├── AppConfig.cs                # JSON config model, load/save, profile matching
121. ├── NativeMethods.cs            # P/Invoke declarations
122. ├── app.manifest                # Execution level manifest
123. └── icon.ico                    # Tray icon
124. 
125. TaskbarAlignmentTool.Package/   # MSIX packaging project (for Microsoft Store)
126. ├── Package.appxmanifest
127. └── Images/                     # Store icon assets
128. ```
129. 
130. ## Future Work
131. 
132. - Per-icon system tray visibility control (show/hide overflow icons per profile)
133. - Multi-monitor support (apply profiles per-monitor, not just primary)
134. 
135. ## License
136. 
137. MIT
138.
````

</details>

1. ---
2. 
3. description: "Task list template for feature implementation"
4. ---
5. 
6. # Tasks: [FEATURE NAME]
7. 
8. **Input**: Design documents from `/specs/[###-feature-name]/`
9. **Prerequisites**: plan.md (required), spec.md (required for user stories), research.md, data-model.md, contracts/
10. 
11. **Tests**: The examples below include test tasks. Tests are OPTIONAL - only include them if explicitly requested in the feature specification.
12. 
13. **Organization**: Tasks are grouped by user story to enable independent implementation and testing of each story.
14. 
15. ## Format: `[ID] [P?] [Story] Description`
16. 
17. - **[P]**: Can run in parallel (different files, no dependencies)
18. - **[Story]**: Which user story this task belongs to (e.g., US1, US2, US3)
19. - Include exact file paths in descriptions
20. 
21. ## Path Conventions
22. 
23. - **Single project**: `src/`, `tests/` at repository root
24. - **Web app**: `backend/src/`, `frontend/src/`
25. - **Mobile**: `api/src/`, `ios/src/` or `android/src/`
26. - Paths shown below assume single project - adjust based on plan.md structure
27. 
28. <!-- 
29.   ============================================================================
30.   IMPORTANT: The tasks below are SAMPLE TASKS for illustration purposes only.
31.   
32.   The /speckit.tasks command MUST replace these with actual tasks based on:
33.   - User stories from spec.md (with their priorities P1, P2, P3...)
34.   - Feature requirements from plan.md
35.   - Entities from data-model.md
36.   - Endpoints from contracts/
37.   
38.   Tasks MUST be organized by user story so each story can be:
39.   - Implemented independently
40.   - Tested independently
41.   - Delivered as an MVP increment
42.   
43.   DO NOT keep these sample tasks in the generated tasks.md file.
44.   ============================================================================
45. -->
46. 
47. ## Phase 1: Setup (Shared Infrastructure)
48. 
49. **Purpose**: Project initialization and basic structure
50. 
51. - [ ] T001 Create project structure per implementation plan
52. - [ ] T002 Initialize [language] project with [framework] dependencies
53. - [ ] T003 [P] Configure linting and formatting tools
54. 
55. ---
56. 
57. ## Phase 2: Foundational (Blocking Prerequisites)
58. 
59. **Purpose**: Core infrastructure that MUST be complete before ANY user story can be implemented
60. 
61. **⚠️ CRITICAL**: No user story work can begin until this phase is complete
62. 
63. Examples of foundational tasks (adjust based on your project):
64. 
65. - [ ] T004 Setup database schema and migrations framework
66. - [ ] T005 [P] Implement authentication/authorization framework
67. - [ ] T006 [P] Setup API routing and middleware structure
68. - [ ] T007 Create base models/entities that all stories depend on
69. - [ ] T008 Configure error handling and logging infrastructure
70. - [ ] T009 Setup environment configuration management
71. 
72. **Checkpoint**: Foundation ready - user story implementation can now begin in parallel
73. 
74. ---
75. 
76. ## Phase 3: User Story 1 - [Title] (Priority: P1) 🎯 MVP
77. 
78. **Goal**: [Brief description of what this story delivers]
79. 
80. **Independent Test**: [How to verify this story works on its own]
81. 
82. ### Tests for User Story 1 (OPTIONAL - only if tests requested) ⚠️
83. 
84. > **NOTE: Write these tests FIRST, ensure they FAIL before implementation**
85. 
86. - [ ] T010 [P] [US1] Contract test for [endpoint] in tests/contract/test_[name].py
87. - [ ] T011 [P] [US1] Integration test for [user journey] in tests/integration/test_[name].py
88. 
89. ### Implementation for User Story 1
90. 
91. - [ ] T012 [P] [US1] Create [Entity1] model in src/models/[entity1].py
92. - [ ] T013 [P] [US1] Create [Entity2] model in src/models/[entity2].py
93. - [ ] T014 [US1] Implement [Service] in src/services/[service].py (depends on T012, T013)
94. - [ ] T015 [US1] Implement [endpoint/feature] in src/[location]/[file].py
95. - [ ] T016 [US1] Add validation and error handling
96. - [ ] T017 [US1] Add logging for user story 1 operations
97. 
98. **Checkpoint**: At this point, User Story 1 should be fully functional and testable independently
99. 
100. ---
101. 
102. ## Phase 4: User Story 2 - [Title] (Priority: P2)
103. 
104. **Goal**: [Brief description of what this story delivers]
105. 
106. **Independent Test**: [How to verify this story works on its own]
107. 
108. ### Tests for User Story 2 (OPTIONAL - only if tests requested) ⚠️
109. 
110. - [ ] T018 [P] [US2] Contract test for [endpoint] in tests/contract/test_[name].py
111. - [ ] T019 [P] [US2] Integration test for [user journey] in tests/integration/test_[name].py
112. 
113. ### Implementation for User Story 2
114. 
115. - [ ] T020 [P] [US2] Create [Entity] model in src/models/[entity].py
116. - [ ] T021 [US2] Implement [Service] in src/services/[service].py
117. - [ ] T022 [US2] Implement [endpoint/feature] in src/[location]/[file].py
118. - [ ] T023 [US2] Integrate with User Story 1 components (if needed)
119. 
120. **Checkpoint**: At this point, User Stories 1 AND 2 should both work independently
121. 
122. ---
123. 
124. ## Phase 5: User Story 3 - [Title] (Priority: P3)
125. 
126. **Goal**: [Brief description of what this story delivers]
127. 
128. **Independent Test**: [How to verify this story works on its own]
129. 
130. ### Tests for User Story 3 (OPTIONAL - only if tests requested) ⚠️
131. 
132. - [ ] T024 [P] [US3] Contract test for [endpoint] in tests/contract/test_[name].py
133. - [ ] T025 [P] [US3] Integration test for [user journey] in tests/integration/test_[name].py
134. 
135. ### Implementation for User Story 3
136. 
137. - [ ] T026 [P] [US3] Create [Entity] model in src/models/[entity].py
138. - [ ] T027 [US3] Implement [Service] in src/services/[service].py
139. - [ ] T028 [US3] Implement [endpoint/feature] in src/[location]/[file].py
140. 
141. **Checkpoint**: All user stories should now be independently functional
142. 
143. ---
144. 
145. [Add more user story phases as needed, following the same pattern]
146. 
147. ---
148. 
149. ## Phase N: Polish & Cross-Cutting Concerns
150. 
151. **Purpose**: Improvements that affect multiple user stories
152. 
153. - [ ] TXXX [P] Documentation updates in docs/
154. - [ ] TXXX Code cleanup and refactoring
155. - [ ] TXXX Performance optimization across all stories
156. - [ ] TXXX [P] Additional unit tests (if requested) in tests/unit/
157. - [ ] TXXX Security hardening
158. - [ ] TXXX Run quickstart.md validation
159. 
160. ---
161. 
162. ## Dependencies & Execution Order
163. 
164. ### Phase Dependencies
165. 
166. - **Setup (Phase 1)**: No dependencies - can start immediately
167. - **Foundational (Phase 2)**: Depends on Setup completion - BLOCKS all user stories
168. - **User Stories (Phase 3+)**: All depend on Foundational phase completion
169.   - User stories can then proceed in parallel (if staffed)
170.   - Or sequentially in priority order (P1 → P2 → P3)
171. - **Polish (Final Phase)**: Depends on all desired user stories being complete
172. 
173. ### User Story Dependencies
174. 
175. - **User Story 1 (P1)**: Can start after Foundational (Phase 2) - No dependencies on other stories
176. - **User Story 2 (P2)**: Can start after Foundational (Phase 2) - May integrate with US1 but should be independently testable
177. - **User Story 3 (P3)**: Can start after Foundational (Phase 2) - May integrate with US1/US2 but should be independently testable
178. 
179. ### Within Each User Story
180. 
181. - Tests (if included) MUST be written and FAIL before implementation
182. - Models before services
183. - Services before endpoints
184. - Core implementation before integration
185. - Story complete before moving to next priority
186. 
187. ### Parallel Opportunities
188. 
189. - All Setup tasks marked [P] can run in parallel
190. - All Foundational tasks marked [P] can run in parallel (within Phase 2)
191. - Once Foundational phase completes, all user stories can start in parallel (if team capacity allows)
192. - All tests for a user story marked [P] can run in parallel
193. - Models within a story marked [P] can run in parallel
194. - Different user stories can be worked on in parallel by different team members
195. 
196. ---
197. 
198. ## Parallel Example: User Story 1
199. 
200. ```bash
201. # Launch all tests for User Story 1 together (if tests requested):
202. Task: "Contract test for [endpoint] in tests/contract/test_[name].py"
203. Task: "Integration test for [user journey] in tests/integration/test_[name].py"
204. 
205. # Launch all models for User Story 1 together:
206. Task: "Create [Entity1] model in src/models/[entity1].py"
207. Task: "Create [Entity2] model in src/models/[entity2].py"
208. ```
209. 
210. ---
211. 
212. ## Implementation Strategy
213. 
214. ### MVP First (User Story 1 Only)
215. 
216. 1. Complete Phase 1: Setup
217. 2. Complete Phase 2: Foundational (CRITICAL - blocks all stories)
218. 3. Complete Phase 3: User Story 1
219. 4. **STOP and VALIDATE**: Test User Story 1 independently
220. 5. Deploy/demo if ready
221. 
222. ### Incremental Delivery
223. 
224. 1. Complete Setup + Foundational → Foundation ready
225. 2. Add User Story 1 → Test independently → Deploy/Demo (MVP!)
226. 3. Add User Story 2 → Test independently → Deploy/Demo
227. 4. Add User Story 3 → Test independently → Deploy/Demo
228. 5. Each story adds value without breaking previous stories
229. 
230. ### Parallel Team Strategy
231. 
232. With multiple developers:
233. 
234. 1. Team completes Setup + Foundational together
235. 2. Once Foundational is done:
236.    - Developer A: User Story 1
237.    - Developer B: User Story 2
238.    - Developer C: User Story 3
239. 3. Stories complete and integrate independently
240. 
241. ---
242. 
243. ## Notes
244. 
245. - [P] tasks = different files, no dependencies
246. - [Story] label maps task to specific user story for traceability
247. - Each user story should be independently completable and testable
248. - Verify tests fail before implementing
249. - Commit after each task or logical group
250. - Stop at any checkpoint to validate story independently
251. - Avoid: vague tasks, same file conflicts, cross-story dependencies that break independence
252.
````

</details>

1. using System.Diagnostics;
2. using Microsoft.Win32;
3. 
4. namespace TaskbarAlignmentTool;
5. 
6. /// <summary>
7. /// Application context that manages the system-tray icon, context menu,
8. /// and wires the display monitor to the taskbar aligner using resolution profiles.
9. /// </summary>
10. internal sealed class TrayApplicationContext : ApplicationContext
11. {
12.     private const string StartupRegistryPath = @"Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run";
13.     private const string StartupValueName = "TaskbarAlignmentTool";
14. 
15.     private AppConfig _config;
16.     private readonly DisplayMonitor _monitor;
17.     private readonly NotifyIcon _notifyIcon;
18.     private readonly ToolStripMenuItem _statusItem;
19.     private readonly ToolStripMenuItem _profileItem;
20.     private readonly ToolStripMenuItem _startupItem;
21. 
22.     private static readonly Lazy<bool> _isMsixPackaged = new(DetectMsixPackaged);
23. 
24.     private int _profileSwitchCount;
25. 
26.     public TrayApplicationContext(AppConfig config)
27.     {
28.         _config = config;
29.         _monitor = new DisplayMonitor(config.RefreshIntervalMs, config.ResolutionMode);
30. 
31.         _statusItem = new ToolStripMenuItem("Initializing...") { Enabled = false };
32.         _profileItem = new ToolStripMenuItem("Profile: —") { Enabled = false };
33.         _startupItem = new ToolStripMenuItem("Run at Startup", null, OnToggleStartup)
34.         {
35.             Checked = IsStartupEnabled()
36.         };
37. 
38.         var menu = new ContextMenuStrip();
39.         menu.Items.Add(_statusItem);
40.         menu.Items.Add(_profileItem);
41.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
42.         menu.Items.Add("Open Config", null, OnOpenConfig);
43.         menu.Items.Add("Reload Config", null, OnReloadConfig);
44.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
45.         menu.Items.Add(_startupItem);
46.         menu.Items.Add("Refresh Now", null, OnRefresh);
47.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
48.         menu.Items.Add(CreateDiagnosticsMenu());
49.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
50.         menu.Items.Add("Exit", null, OnExit);
51. 
52.         _notifyIcon = new NotifyIcon
53.         {
54.             Icon = RenderTrayIcon(0),
55.             ContextMenuStrip = menu,
56.             Text = "Taskbar Alignment Tool",
57.             Visible = true
58.         };
59. 
60.         _monitor.ResolutionChanged += OnResolutionChanged;
61. 
62.         // Apply profile immediately on startup
63.         ApplyForWidth(_monitor.EffectiveWidth);
64.     }
65. 
66.     private void OnResolutionChanged(object? sender, int effectiveWidth)
67.     {
68.         ApplyForWidth(effectiveWidth);
69.     }
70. 
71.     private void ApplyForWidth(int effectiveWidth)
72.     {
73.         var profile = _config.ResolveProfile(effectiveWidth);
74.         if (profile != null && TaskbarAligner.ApplyProfile(profile))
75.         {
76.             _profileSwitchCount++;
77.             if (_config.ShowNotifications)
78.             {
79.                 _notifyIcon.ShowBalloonTip(
80.                     _config.NotificationDurationMs,
81.                     "Taskbar Alignment Tool",
82.                     $"Switched to \"{profile.Name}\" ({effectiveWidth}px)",
83.                     ToolTipIcon.Info);
84.             }
85.         }
86.         UpdateStatus(effectiveWidth, profile);
87.     }
88. 
89.     private void UpdateStatus(int effectiveWidth, ProfileConfig? profile)
90.     {
91.         var profileName = profile?.Name ?? "None";
92.         _statusItem.Text = $"Width: {effectiveWidth}px";
93.         _profileItem.Text = $"Profile: {profileName}";
94.         _notifyIcon.Text = $"Taskbar: {profileName} ({effectiveWidth}px)";
95. 
96.         // Update tray icon with current width
97.         var oldIcon = _notifyIcon.Icon;
98.         _notifyIcon.Icon = RenderTrayIcon(effectiveWidth);
99.         oldIcon?.Dispose();
100.     }
101. 
102.     private void OnRefresh(object? sender, EventArgs e)
103.     {
104.         _monitor.Refresh(force: true);
105.     }
106. 
107.     private void OnOpenConfig(object? sender, EventArgs e)
108.     {
109.         var path = AppConfig.GetConfigPath();
110.         if (!File.Exists(path))
111.             _config.Save();
112.         Process.Start(new ProcessStartInfo(path) { UseShellExecute = true });
113.     }
114. 
115.     private void OnReloadConfig(object? sender, EventArgs e)
116.     {
117.         _config = AppConfig.Load();
118.         _monitor.UpdateInterval(_config.RefreshIntervalMs, _config.ResolutionMode);
119.         _monitor.Refresh(force: true);
120.     }
121. 
122.     private void OnToggleStartup(object? sender, EventArgs e)
123.     {
124.         if (IsStartupEnabled())
125.             DisableStartup();
126.         else
127.             EnableStartup();
128. 
129.         _startupItem.Checked = IsStartupEnabled();
130.     }
131. 
132.     private static bool IsMsixPackaged() => _isMsixPackaged.Value;
133. 
134.     private static bool DetectMsixPackaged()
135.     {
136.         try
137.         {
138.             // Windows.ApplicationModel.Package.Current throws if not packaged
139.             _ = Windows.ApplicationModel.Package.Current.Id;
140.             return true;
141.         }
142.         catch
143.         {
144.             return false;
145.         }
146.     }
147. 
148.     private static bool IsStartupEnabled()
149.     {
150.         if (IsMsixPackaged())
151.         {
152.             try
153.             {
154.                 var task = Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTask
155.                     .GetAsync("TaskbarAlignmentToolStartup").GetAwaiter().GetResult();
156.                 return task.State == Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTaskState.Enabled;
157.             }
158.             catch { return false; }
159.         }
160. 
161.         using var key = Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(StartupRegistryPath, false);
162.         return key?.GetValue(StartupValueName) != null;
163.     }
164. 
165.     private static void EnableStartup()
166.     {
167.         if (IsMsixPackaged())
168.         {
169.             try
170.             {
171.                 var task = Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTask
172.                     .GetAsync("TaskbarAlignmentToolStartup").GetAwaiter().GetResult();
173.                 task.RequestEnableAsync().GetAwaiter().GetResult();
174.             }
175.             catch { /* Startup task not available */ }
176.             return;
177.         }
178. 
179.         using var key = Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(StartupRegistryPath, true);
180.         var exePath = Application.ExecutablePath;
181.         key?.SetValue(StartupValueName, $"\"{exePath}\"");
182.     }
183. 
184.     private static void DisableStartup()
185.     {
186.         if (IsMsixPackaged())
187.         {
188.             try
189.             {
190.                 var task = Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTask
191.                     .GetAsync("TaskbarAlignmentToolStartup").GetAwaiter().GetResult();
192.                 task.Disable();
193.             }
194.             catch { /* Startup task not available */ }
195.             return;
196.         }
197. 
198.         using var key = Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(StartupRegistryPath, true);
199.         key?.DeleteValue(StartupValueName, false);
200.     }
201. 
202.     private ToolStripMenuItem CreateDiagnosticsMenu()
203.     {
204.         var diag = new ToolStripMenuItem("Diagnostics");
205.         diag.DropDownOpening += (_, _) =>
206.         {
207.             diag.DropDownItems.Clear();
208.             var proc = System.Diagnostics.Process.GetCurrentProcess();
209.             var memMb = proc.WorkingSet64 / (1024.0 * 1024.0);
210.             var cpuTime = proc.TotalProcessorTime;
211.             diag.DropDownItems.Add(new ToolStripMenuItem($"Memory: {memMb:F1} MB") { Enabled = false });
212.             diag.DropDownItems.Add(new ToolStripMenuItem($"CPU time: {cpuTime.TotalSeconds:F2}s") { Enabled = false });
213.             diag.DropDownItems.Add(new ToolStripMenuItem($"Profile switches: {_profileSwitchCount}") { Enabled = false });
214.         };
215.         return diag;
216.     }

220.         _notifyIcon.Visible = false;
221.         _monitor.Dispose();
222.         _notifyIcon.Dispose();
223.         Application.Exit();
224.     }

226.     /// <summary>
227.     /// Renders a DPI-aware tray icon showing the current display width.
228.     /// Auto-detects Windows light/dark theme for contrast.
229.     /// </summary>
230.     private static Icon RenderTrayIcon(int width)
231.     {
232.         int dpi = GetSystemDpi();
233.         int size = (int)(16 * dpi / 96.0);
234.         float scale = size / 16f;
235.         bool isDarkTheme = IsSystemDarkTheme();
236. 
237.         using var bmp = new Bitmap(size, size);
238.         using var g = Graphics.FromImage(bmp);
239.         g.SmoothingMode = System.Drawing.Drawing2D.SmoothingMode.HighQuality;
240.         g.TextRenderingHint = System.Drawing.Text.TextRenderingHint.ClearTypeGridFit;
241.         g.PixelOffsetMode = System.Drawing.Drawing2D.PixelOffsetMode.HighQuality;
242.         g.Clear(Color.Transparent);
243. 
244.         // Background color adapts to theme
245.         var bgColor = isDarkTheme
246.             ? Color.FromArgb(255, 0, 150, 180)   // Teal on dark taskbar
247.             : Color.FromArgb(255, 0, 120, 150);  // Darker teal on light taskbar
248.         var textBrush = isDarkTheme ? Brushes.White : Brushes.White;
249. 
250.         using var bgBrush = new SolidBrush(bgColor);
251.         g.FillRectangle(bgBrush, 0, 0, size, size);
252. 
253.         // Display width as the full icon content
254.         var label = width.ToString();
255.         float fontSize = label.Length <= 3 ? 7f : label.Length == 4 ? 5.5f : 4.5f;
256.         using var font = new Font("Segoe UI", fontSize * scale, FontStyle.Bold, GraphicsUnit.Pixel);
257.         var sf = new StringFormat
258.         {
259.             Alignment = StringAlignment.Center,
260.             LineAlignment = StringAlignment.Center
261.         };
262.         var textRect = new RectangleF(0, 0, size, size);
263.         g.DrawString(label, font, textBrush, textRect, sf);
264. 
265.         return Icon.FromHandle(bmp.GetHicon());
266.     }
267. 
268.     /// <summary>
269.     /// Checks if Windows is using dark mode for apps.
270.     /// Registry: HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Themes\Personalize\SystemUsesLightTheme
271.     /// 0 = dark, 1 = light
272.     /// </summary>
273.     private static bool IsSystemDarkTheme()
274.     {
275.         try
276.         {
277.             using var key = Microsoft.Win32.Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(
278.                 @"Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Themes\Personalize", false);
279.             var value = key?.GetValue("SystemUsesLightTheme");
280.             if (value is int intVal)
281.                 return intVal == 0;
282.         }
283.         catch { }
284.         return true; // Default to dark
285.     }
286. 
287.     private static int GetSystemDpi()
288.     {
289.         try
290.         {
291.             var hMonitor = NativeMethods.MonitorFromPoint(0, NativeMethods.MONITOR_DEFAULTTOPRIMARY);
292.             if (hMonitor != nint.Zero &&
293.                 NativeMethods.GetDpiForMonitor(hMonitor, NativeMethods.MDT_EFFECTIVE_DPI, out uint dpiX, out _) == 0 &&
294.                 dpiX > 0)
295.             {
296.                 return (int)dpiX;
297.             }
298.         }
299.         catch { }
300.         return 96;
301.     }
302. }
303. 
```

1. using Microsoft.Win32;
2. 
3. namespace TaskbarAlignmentTool;
4. 
5. /// <summary>
6. /// Monitors the primary display's effective resolution using Windows messages
7. /// (WM_DISPLAYCHANGE, WM_DPICHANGED) and a low-frequency fallback timer.
8. /// </summary>
9. internal sealed class DisplayMonitor : IDisposable
10. {
11.     private readonly System.Windows.Forms.Timer _fallbackTimer;
12.     private readonly DisplayChangeWindow _messageWindow;
13.     private int _lastWidth;
14.     private ResolutionMode _resolutionMode;
15. 
16.     public int EffectiveWidth => GetWidth(_resolutionMode);
17. 
18.     public event EventHandler<int>? ResolutionChanged;
19. 
20.     public DisplayMonitor(int fallbackIntervalMs = 60000, ResolutionMode resolutionMode = ResolutionMode.Effective)
21.     {
22.         _resolutionMode = resolutionMode;
23.         _lastWidth = EffectiveWidth;
24. 
25.         // Hidden window to receive WM_DISPLAYCHANGE and WM_DPICHANGED
26.         _messageWindow = new DisplayChangeWindow(OnDisplayMessage);
27. 
28.         // Also subscribe to the .NET event for display settings changes
29.         SystemEvents.DisplaySettingsChanged += OnDisplaySettingsChanged;
30. 
31.         // Low-frequency fallback timer (default 60s) as a safety net
32.         _fallbackTimer = new System.Windows.Forms.Timer
33.         {
34.             Interval = Math.Max(fallbackIntervalMs, 5000)
35.         };
36.         _fallbackTimer.Tick += OnFallbackTick;
37.         _fallbackTimer.Start();
38.     }
39. 
40.     /// <summary>Updates the fallback timer interval and resolution mode at runtime.</summary>
41.     public void UpdateInterval(int intervalMs, ResolutionMode? resolutionMode = null)
42.     {
43.         _fallbackTimer.Interval = Math.Max(intervalMs, 5000);
44.         if (resolutionMode.HasValue)
45.             _resolutionMode = resolutionMode.Value;
46.     }
47. 
48.     private void OnDisplayMessage()
49.     {
50.         CheckAndNotify();
51.     }
52. 
53.     private void OnDisplaySettingsChanged(object? sender, EventArgs e)
54.     {
55.         CheckAndNotify();
56.     }
57. 
58.     private void OnFallbackTick(object? sender, EventArgs e)
59.     {
60.         CheckAndNotify();
61.     }
62. 
63.     private void CheckAndNotify()
64.     {
65.         var current = EffectiveWidth;
66.         if (current != _lastWidth)
67.         {
68.             _lastWidth = current;
69.             ResolutionChanged?.Invoke(this, current);
70.         }
71.     }
72. 
73.     /// <summary>Forces a re-check and fires the event if the width changed (or always if force is true).</summary>
74.     public void Refresh(bool force = false)
75.     {
76.         var current = EffectiveWidth;
77.         if (force || current != _lastWidth)
78.         {
79.             _lastWidth = current;
80.             ResolutionChanged?.Invoke(this, current);
81.         }
82.     }
83. 
84.     private static int GetWidth(ResolutionMode mode)
85.     {
86.         var screen = Screen.PrimaryScreen;
87.         if (screen == null) return 0;
88. 
89.         int physicalWidth = screen.Bounds.Width;
90. 
91.         if (mode == ResolutionMode.Physical)
92.             return physicalWidth;
93. 
94.         try
95.         {
96.             var hMonitor = NativeMethods.MonitorFromPoint(0, NativeMethods.MONITOR_DEFAULTTOPRIMARY);
97.             if (hMonitor != nint.Zero &&
98.                 NativeMethods.GetDpiForMonitor(hMonitor, NativeMethods.MDT_EFFECTIVE_DPI, out uint dpiX, out _) == 0 &&
99.                 dpiX > 0)
100.             {
101.                 return (int)(physicalWidth * 96.0 / dpiX);
102.             }
103.         }
104.         catch
105.         {
106.             // Fall through to physical width
107.         }
108. 
109.         return physicalWidth;
110.     }
111. 
112.     public void Dispose()
113.     {
114.         SystemEvents.DisplaySettingsChanged -= OnDisplaySettingsChanged;
115.         _fallbackTimer.Stop();
116.         _fallbackTimer.Dispose();
117.         _messageWindow.DestroyHandle();
118.     }
119. 
120.     /// <summary>
121.     /// Hidden NativeWindow that receives WM_DISPLAYCHANGE and WM_DPICHANGED messages.
122.     /// </summary>
123.     private sealed class DisplayChangeWindow : NativeWindow
124.     {
125.         private readonly Action _onChange;
126. 
127.         public DisplayChangeWindow(Action onChange)
128.         {
129.             _onChange = onChange;
130.             var cp = new CreateParams
131.             {
132.                 Caption = "TaskbarAlignmentTool_DisplayMonitor",
133.                 // Message-only window (HWND_MESSAGE parent)
134.                 Parent = new nint(-3)
135.             };
136.             CreateHandle(cp);
137.         }
138. 
139.         protected override void WndProc(ref Message m)
140.         {
141.             if (m.Msg is NativeMethods.WM_DISPLAYCHANGE or NativeMethods.WM_DPICHANGED)
142.             {
143.                 _onChange();
144.             }
145.             base.WndProc(ref m);
146.         }
147.     }
148. }
149. 
```


### 💬 Copilot

## Answers to your questions:

### 1. **Tray Icon Hover Tooltip**
The tooltip shows: **`"Taskbar: {profileName} ({effectiveWidth}px)"`**
- Line 94: `_notifyIcon.Text = $"Taskbar: {profileName} ({effectiveWidth}px)";`
- Example: "Taskbar: Ultrawide (2560px)"

### 2. **How DisplayMonitor Tracks Primary Display Resolution**
Uses three-layered approach:
- **Primary source**: Hidden message window (`DisplayChangeWindow`) receives `WM_DISPLAYCHANGE` and `WM_DPICHANGED` Windows messages (lines 141-144)
- **Secondary source**: `SystemEvents.DisplaySettingsChanged` .NET event (line 29)
- **Fallback**: Low-frequency timer (default 60s) checks periodically (lines 32-37)
- All trigger `CheckAndNotify()` which compares current vs. last width

### 3. **Display Scaling / DPI Awareness**
✅ **Yes, fully aware.** The app handles both:
- **Effective width** (DPI-scaled): Calculated via `GetDpiForMonitor()` native call (lines 96-102)
  - Formula: `physicalWidth * 96.0 / dpiX` (converts physical to logical pixels)
- **Physical width** (raw): Direct `Screen.PrimaryScreen.Bounds.Width` (line 89)
- **Configurable mode** via `ResolutionMode` enum to use either
- **Tray icon scaling** (lines 232-233): Scales icon size based on system DPI

### 4. **Tray Context Menu Structure**
```
├─ Status (disabled): "Width: {effectiveWidth}px"
├─ Profile (disabled): "Profile: {profileName}"
├─ ─────────────────
├─ Open Config
├─ Reload Config
├─ ─────────────────
├─ Run at Startup (checked/unchecked)
├─ Refresh Now
├─ ─────────────────
├─ Diagnostics (submenu: Memory, CPU time, Profile switches)
├─ ─────────────────
└─ Exit
```
(Lines 38-50)

1. # Feature Specification: Hover Effective Resolution
2. 
3. **Feature Branch**: `001-hover-effective-resolution`
4. **Created**: 2026-03-06
5. **Status**: Draft
6. **Input**: User description: "I'd like the hover menu to include the current effective resolution — actual resolution reduced by scaling (125%, 200%, etc)."
7. 
8. ## User Scenarios & Testing *(mandatory)*
9. 
10. ### User Story 1 — See Effective Resolution on Hover (Priority: P1)
11. 
12. A user docks their laptop to an external monitor and wants to quickly
13. confirm what effective resolution the tool is using to select a
14. profile. They hover over the tray icon and immediately see the
15. effective resolution alongside the native resolution and current
16. scaling factor without opening any menu.
17. 
18. **Why this priority**: This is the entire feature request. Showing
19. the effective resolution on hover gives the user instant visibility
20. into why a particular profile was chosen, which is the core value.
21. 
22. **Independent Test**: Hover over the tray icon on a display with
23. non-100% scaling. Verify the tooltip shows effective resolution,
24. native resolution, and scaling percentage, all matching the actual
25. display settings reported by Windows Display Settings.
26. 
27. **Acceptance Scenarios**:
28. 
29. 1. **Given** the app is running on a 3840×2160 display at 200%
30.    scaling, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
31.    the tooltip displays the effective width (1920 px), the native
32.    width (3840 px), and the scaling factor (200%).
33. 2. **Given** the app is running on a 1920×1080 display at 100%
34.    scaling, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
35.    the tooltip displays the effective width (1920 px) and indicates
36.    no scaling is applied (100%).
37. 3. **Given** the user undocks from an external monitor and the
38.    laptop's built-in 2560×1600 display at 150% scaling becomes
39.    primary, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
40.    the tooltip updates to reflect the laptop display's effective
41.    width (1707 px), native width (2560 px), and scaling (150%).
42. 
43. ---
44. 
45. ### User Story 2 — Resolution Details in Context Menu (Priority: P2)
46. 
47. A user right-clicks the tray icon and sees the current resolution
48. breakdown in the status area of the context menu, providing a
49. persistent reference that doesn't disappear like a tooltip does.
50. 
51. **Why this priority**: Complements the tooltip by surfacing the same
52. information in the context menu where users already look for status.
53. The existing "Width:" menu item already shows effective width; this
54. story enriches it with native resolution and scaling factor.
55. 
56. **Independent Test**: Right-click the tray icon. Verify the status
57. area shows effective resolution, native resolution, and scaling
58. percentage matching the actual display settings.
59. 
60. **Acceptance Scenarios**:
61. 
62. 1. **Given** the app is running on a 3840×2160 display at 200%
63.    scaling, **When** the user right-clicks the tray icon, **Then**
64.    the context menu status shows effective width (1920 px), native
65.    width (3840 px), and scaling (200%).
66. 2. **Given** the display scaling changes while the app is running,
67.    **When** the user right-clicks the tray icon, **Then** the
68.    context menu reflects the updated values on next poll cycle.
69. 
70. ---
71. 
72. ### Edge Cases
73. 
74. - What happens when the scaling factor is a non-standard value
75.   (e.g., 125%, 175%)? The displayed percentage MUST match the
76.   actual system DPI scaling, rounded to the nearest whole percent.
77. - What happens when the primary display cannot be detected (e.g.,
78.   headless or remote desktop with no physical monitor)? The tooltip
79.   MUST show a graceful fallback message such as "Resolution:
80.   unavailable" rather than crashing or showing stale data.
81. - What happens when effective and native resolution are identical
82.   (100% scaling)? The tooltip MUST still show both values and
83.   indicate 100% scaling for consistency.
84. - What happens with fractional scaling values like 137.5%? The
85.   display MUST round the percentage to the nearest whole number
86.   and the effective width to the nearest whole pixel.
87. 
88. ## Requirements *(mandatory)*
89. 
90. ### Functional Requirements
91. 
92. - **FR-001**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the current
93.   effective width in pixels (the value used for profile matching).
94. - **FR-002**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the native
95.   (physical) width of the primary display in pixels.
96. - **FR-003**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the current display
97.   scaling factor as a percentage (e.g., "125%", "200%").
98. - **FR-004**: The context menu status area MUST display the same
99.   effective width, native width, and scaling percentage as the
100.   tooltip.
101. - **FR-005**: All displayed resolution values MUST update
102.   automatically when the primary display changes (monitor
103.   switch, dock/undock, scaling change) within the existing poll
104.   interval.
105. - **FR-006**: The tooltip MUST remain within the operating system's
106.   tooltip character limit (currently 128 characters on Windows).
107. - **FR-007**: The tooltip and context menu MUST display whole-number
108.   pixel values and whole-number percentages (rounding to nearest).
109. 
110. ### Key Entities
111. 
112. - **Display Info**: Represents the current primary display state —
113.   native width (physical pixels), effective width (logical pixels
114.   after scaling), and scaling factor (percentage).
115. 
116. ## Success Criteria *(mandatory)*
117. 
118. ### Measurable Outcomes
119. 
120. - **SC-001**: Users can determine the effective resolution, native
121.   resolution, and scaling factor of their primary display by
122.   hovering over the tray icon, without opening any menu or
123.   settings panel.
124. - **SC-002**: The displayed resolution values match the values shown
125.   in Windows Display Settings for the primary monitor in 100% of
126.   tested scaling configurations (100%, 125%, 150%, 175%, 200%,
127.   225%, 250%, 300%).
128. - **SC-003**: Resolution information updates within one poll cycle
129.   (default 5 seconds) after a display change event (dock, undock,
130.   scaling change).
131. - **SC-004**: The tooltip text fits within the system tooltip
132.   character limit and is fully readable without truncation.
133. 
134. ## Assumptions
135. 
136. - The tooltip format will follow a pattern like:
137.   `"Profile: Ultrawide | 1920 px effective (3840 px @ 200%)"` —
138.   exact wording may be refined during implementation as long as all
139.   three data points (effective, native, scaling) are present.
140. - The existing poll/event mechanism for detecting display changes is
141.   sufficient; no new detection mechanism is needed.
142. - Only the primary display's resolution is shown (multi-monitor
143.   display is out of scope for this feature).
144.
```

</details>

### 💬 Copilot

I've loaded the spec and performed the full ambiguity/coverage scan. Here's my assessment:

**Coverage scan results**: This is a narrow, well-defined feature. The spec covers all critical categories — core goals, edge cases, data model, error states, and acceptance criteria are all concrete and testable. No `[NEEDS CLARIFICATION]` markers exist, and no vague adjectives need quantification.

I identified two minor partial-coverage areas, but neither warrants a formal question:

1. **US2 (context menu) scope** — the user asked about "hover" only; the spec proactively added context menu enrichment as P2. This is a reasonable extension, not an ambiguity, and planning can scope it up or down.
2. **Width-only vs WxH** — the user said "resolution" but this tool is fundamentally width-based for profile matching. Width-only is the clear default given the app's design. Both would fit the 128-char tooltip limit, but height adds no functional value here.

**No critical ambiguities detected worth formal clarification.** The spec is ready to advance.

<details>
<summary>144 lines</summary>

```
1. # Feature Specification: Hover Effective Resolution
2. 
3. **Feature Branch**: `001-hover-effective-resolution`
4. **Created**: 2026-03-06
5. **Status**: Draft
6. **Input**: User description: "I'd like the hover menu to include the current effective resolution — actual resolution reduced by scaling (125%, 200%, etc)."
7. 
8. ## User Scenarios & Testing *(mandatory)*
9. 
10. ### User Story 1 — See Effective Resolution on Hover (Priority: P1)
11. 
12. A user docks their laptop to an external monitor and wants to quickly
13. confirm what effective resolution the tool is using to select a
14. profile. They hover over the tray icon and immediately see the
15. effective resolution alongside the native resolution and current
16. scaling factor without opening any menu.
17. 
18. **Why this priority**: This is the entire feature request. Showing
19. the effective resolution on hover gives the user instant visibility
20. into why a particular profile was chosen, which is the core value.
21. 
22. **Independent Test**: Hover over the tray icon on a display with
23. non-100% scaling. Verify the tooltip shows effective resolution,
24. native resolution, and scaling percentage, all matching the actual
25. display settings reported by Windows Display Settings.
26. 
27. **Acceptance Scenarios**:
28. 
29. 1. **Given** the app is running on a 3840×2160 display at 200%
30.    scaling, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
31.    the tooltip displays the effective width (1920 px), the native
32.    width (3840 px), and the scaling factor (200%).
33. 2. **Given** the app is running on a 1920×1080 display at 100%
34.    scaling, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
35.    the tooltip displays the effective width (1920 px) and indicates
36.    no scaling is applied (100%).
37. 3. **Given** the user undocks from an external monitor and the
38.    laptop's built-in 2560×1600 display at 150% scaling becomes
39.    primary, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
40.    the tooltip updates to reflect the laptop display's effective
41.    width (1707 px), native width (2560 px), and scaling (150%).
42. 
43. ---
44. 
45. ### User Story 2 — Resolution Details in Context Menu (Priority: P2)
46. 
47. A user right-clicks the tray icon and sees the current resolution
48. breakdown in the status area of the context menu, providing a
49. persistent reference that doesn't disappear like a tooltip does.
50. 
51. **Why this priority**: Complements the tooltip by surfacing the same
52. information in the context menu where users already look for status.
53. The existing "Width:" menu item already shows effective width; this
54. story enriches it with native resolution and scaling factor.
55. 
56. **Independent Test**: Right-click the tray icon. Verify the status
57. area shows effective resolution, native resolution, and scaling
58. percentage matching the actual display settings.
59. 
60. **Acceptance Scenarios**:
61. 
62. 1. **Given** the app is running on a 3840×2160 display at 200%
63.    scaling, **When** the user right-clicks the tray icon, **Then**
64.    the context menu status shows effective width (1920 px), native
65.    width (3840 px), and scaling (200%).
66. 2. **Given** the display scaling changes while the app is running,
67.    **When** the user right-clicks the tray icon, **Then** the
68.    context menu reflects the updated values on next poll cycle.
69. 
70. ---
71. 
72. ### Edge Cases
73. 
74. - What happens when the scaling factor is a non-standard value
75.   (e.g., 125%, 175%)? The displayed percentage MUST match the
76.   actual system DPI scaling, rounded to the nearest whole percent.
77. - What happens when the primary display cannot be detected (e.g.,
78.   headless or remote desktop with no physical monitor)? The tooltip
79.   MUST show a graceful fallback message such as "Resolution:
80.   unavailable" rather than crashing or showing stale data.
81. - What happens when effective and native resolution are identical
82.   (100% scaling)? The tooltip MUST still show both values and
83.   indicate 100% scaling for consistency.
84. - What happens with fractional scaling values like 137.5%? The
85.   display MUST round the percentage to the nearest whole number
86.   and the effective width to the nearest whole pixel.
87. 
88. ## Requirements *(mandatory)*
89. 
90. ### Functional Requirements
91. 
92. - **FR-001**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the current
93.   effective width in pixels (the value used for profile matching).
94. - **FR-002**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the native
95.   (physical) width of the primary display in pixels.
96. - **FR-003**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the current display
97.   scaling factor as a percentage (e.g., "125%", "200%").
98. - **FR-004**: The context menu status area MUST display the same
99.   effective width, native width, and scaling percentage as the
100.   tooltip.
101. - **FR-005**: All displayed resolution values MUST update
102.   automatically when the primary display changes (monitor
103.   switch, dock/undock, scaling change) within the existing poll
104.   interval.
105. - **FR-006**: The tooltip MUST remain within the operating system's
106.   tooltip character limit (currently 128 characters on Windows).
107. - **FR-007**: The tooltip and context menu MUST display whole-number
108.   pixel values and whole-number percentages (rounding to nearest).
109. 
110. ### Key Entities
111. 
112. - **Display Info**: Represents the current primary display state —
113.   native width (physical pixels), effective width (logical pixels
114.   after scaling), and scaling factor (percentage).
115. 
116. ## Success Criteria *(mandatory)*
117. 
118. ### Measurable Outcomes
119. 
120. - **SC-001**: Users can determine the effective resolution, native
121.   resolution, and scaling factor of their primary display by
122.   hovering over the tray icon, without opening any menu or
123.   settings panel.
124. - **SC-002**: The displayed resolution values match the values shown
125.   in Windows Display Settings for the primary monitor in 100% of
126.   tested scaling configurations (100%, 125%, 150%, 175%, 200%,
127.   225%, 250%, 300%).
128. - **SC-003**: Resolution information updates within one poll cycle
129.   (default 5 seconds) after a display change event (dock, undock,
130.   scaling change).
131. - **SC-004**: The tooltip text fits within the system tooltip
132.   character limit and is fully readable without truncation.
133. 
134. ## Assumptions
135. 
136. - The tooltip format will follow a pattern like:
137.   `"Profile: Ultrawide | 1920 px effective (3840 px @ 200%)"` —
138.   exact wording may be refined during implementation as long as all
139.   three data points (effective, native, scaling) are present.
140. - The existing poll/event mechanism for detecting display changes is
141.   sufficient; no new detection mechanism is needed.
142. - Only the primary display's resolution is shown (multi-monitor
143.   display is out of scope for this feature).
144.
```

</details>

19. 
20. # Taskbar Alignment Tool Constitution
21. 
22. ## Core Principles
23. 
24. ### I. Lightweight
25. 
26. The tool MUST remain minimal in binary size and runtime resource
27. consumption. Background polling MUST use negligible CPU when idle.
28. Memory footprint MUST stay under 30 MB during normal operation.
29. New features MUST NOT introduce heavy frameworks or large dependency
30. trees. If a capability can be achieved with a smaller approach, the
31. smaller approach wins.
32. 
33. **Rationale**: This is a background utility that users forget is
34. running. It MUST behave like one.
35. 
36. ### II. Self-Contained
37. 
38. The published artifact MUST be fully portable — a single executable
39. or MSIX package that requires no pre-installed runtimes, no external
40. services, and no network access. All configuration MUST be local
41. (`%LOCALAPPDATA%`). The tool MUST function identically whether run
42. from a USB drive, a fresh Windows install, or the Microsoft Store.
43. 
44. **Rationale**: Users expect system tray utilities to "just work"
45. without setup ceremonies.
46. 
47. ### III. Pragmatic Code
48. 
49. Working, readable code is valued over architectural purity. Patterns
50. and abstractions MUST justify themselves by solving a concrete
51. problem — not by satisfying a design principle. Refactoring is
52. welcome when it reduces complexity, but MUST NOT be pursued for
53. its own sake. "Good enough and shipping" beats "perfect and
54. stalled."
55. 
56. **Rationale**: This is a small single-purpose tool. Over-engineering
57. increases maintenance burden without user benefit.
58. 
59. ### IV. Startup-Ready
60. 
61. The tool MUST support automatic launch at user logon via MSIX
62. StartupTask (preferred) or `HKCU` registry entry (fallback). Startup
63. registration and deregistration MUST be toggleable from the system
64. tray context menu without requiring administrator privileges. First
65. launch MUST NOT require user interaction — sensible defaults apply
66. immediately.
67. 
68. **Rationale**: The tool's value comes from running continuously and
69. invisibly. Manual launch defeats the purpose.
70. 
71. ### V. Store-Distributable
72. 
73. All code and packaging decisions MUST keep Microsoft Store
74. distribution viable. The app MUST pass MSIX/WACK validation. Only
75. `HKCU` registry writes are permitted (no `HKLM`, no admin elevation).
76. P/Invoke calls MUST be limited to documented, Store-compatible Win32
77. APIs. The MSIX packaging project MUST be kept in sync with the main
78. application project.
79. 
80. **Rationale**: Store distribution provides auto-updates, trust
81. signals, and discoverability that a raw `.exe` cannot match.
82. 
83. ## Technical Constraints
84. 
85. - **Platform**: Windows 11 only (relies on Windows 11-specific
86.   registry keys `TaskbarAl`, `TaskbarGlomLevel`, `TaskbarSi`)
87. - **Runtime**: .NET 8+ (self-contained publish eliminates runtime
88.   dependency for end users)
89. - **UI surface**: System tray icon + context menu only — no main
90.   window unless explicitly justified
91. - **Registry scope**: `HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\Advanced`
92.   exclusively — never `HKLM`
93. - **Configuration**: Single JSON file in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\TaskbarAlignmentTool\`
94. - **Privileges**: Standard user only — MUST NOT require or request
95.   elevation
96. 
97. ## Distribution & Packaging
98. 
99. - **Primary channel**: MSIX package via `TaskbarAlignmentTool.Package`
100.   project, targeting Microsoft Store submission
101. - **Secondary channel**: Self-contained single-file publish
102.   (`dotnet publish -r win-x64 --self-contained`) for sideloading
103. - **Startup**: MSIX `StartupTask` declaration in `Package.appxmanifest`;
104.   registry fallback for non-packaged builds
105. - **Assets**: Store icon assets MUST be maintained in
106.   `TaskbarAlignmentTool.Package\Images\`
107. - **Versioning**: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH following semver; package version
108.   in `Package.appxmanifest` MUST match assembly version
109. 
110. ## Governance
111. 
112. This constitution is the authoritative source of project direction.
113. All changes — features, refactors, dependency additions — MUST be
114. evaluated against these principles before implementation.
115. 
116. - **Amendments**: Any principle change MUST be documented with a
117.   rationale, reflected in a version bump, and propagated to dependent
118.   templates in `.specify/templates/`.
119. - **Versioning**: Constitution follows semantic versioning. Principle
120.   removals or redefinitions are MAJOR. New principles or sections are
121.   MINOR. Wording and clarification changes are PATCH.
122. - **Compliance**: Feature specs and implementation plans MUST include
123.   a constitution check confirming alignment with all five principles.
124.   Violations MUST be justified in a complexity tracking table.
125. 
126. **Version**: 1.0.0 | **Ratified**: 2026-03-06 | **Last Amended**: 2026-03-06
127.
```

1. # Feature Specification: Hover Effective Resolution
2. 
3. **Feature Branch**: `001-hover-effective-resolution`
4. **Created**: 2026-03-06
5. **Status**: Draft
6. **Input**: User description: "Show effective resolution (W×H) in
7. tray tooltip on hover AND at the top of the right-click context
8. menu. Effective = native resolution reduced by display scaling."
9. 
10. ## User Scenarios & Testing *(mandatory)*
11. 
12. ### User Story 1 — See Effective Resolution on Hover (Priority: P1)
13. 
14. A user docks their laptop to an external monitor and wants to
15. quickly confirm what effective resolution the tool is using to
16. select a profile. They hover over the tray icon and immediately
17. see the full effective resolution (W×H), the native resolution
18. (W×H), and the current scaling factor — without opening any menu.
19. 
20. **Why this priority**: The tooltip is the fastest way to check
21. resolution at a glance. Showing W×H (not just width) gives
22. complete context that matches how users think about resolution.
23. 
24. **Independent Test**: Hover over the tray icon on a display with
25. non-100% scaling. Verify the tooltip shows effective W×H, native
26. W×H, and scaling percentage, all matching Windows Display Settings.
27. 
28. **Acceptance Scenarios**:
29. 
30. 1. **Given** the app is running on a 3840×2160 display at 200%
31.    scaling, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
32.    the tooltip displays effective resolution (1920×1080), native
33.    resolution (3840×2160), and the scaling factor (200%).
34. 2. **Given** the app is running on a 1920×1080 display at 100%
35.    scaling, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
36.    the tooltip displays effective resolution (1920×1080) and
37.    indicates scaling is 100%.
38. 3. **Given** the user undocks from an external monitor and the
39.    laptop's built-in 2560×1600 display at 150% scaling becomes
40.    primary, **When** the user hovers over the tray icon, **Then**
41.    the tooltip updates to show effective resolution (1707×1067),
42.    native resolution (2560×1600), and scaling (150%).
43. 
44. ---
45. 
46. ### User Story 2 — Resolution Details in Context Menu (Priority: P1)
47. 
48. A user right-clicks the tray icon and sees the current resolution
49. breakdown at the top of the context menu, providing a persistent
50. reference that does not disappear like a tooltip. This information
51. appears above the existing menu items.
52. 
53. **Why this priority**: The user explicitly requested the info on
54. both hover and click. The context menu is the primary interaction
55. surface; resolution details at the top give immediate visibility
56. every time the menu opens.
57. 
58. **Independent Test**: Right-click the tray icon. Verify the top of
59. the context menu shows effective W×H, native W×H, and scaling
60. percentage matching the actual display settings.
61. 
62. **Acceptance Scenarios**:
63. 
64. 1. **Given** the app is running on a 3840×2160 display at 200%
65.    scaling, **When** the user right-clicks the tray icon, **Then**
66.    the top of the context menu shows effective resolution
67.    (1920×1080), native resolution (3840×2160), and scaling (200%).
68. 2. **Given** the display scaling changes while the app is running,
69.    **When** the user right-clicks the tray icon, **Then** the
70.    context menu reflects the updated values on next poll cycle.
71. 3. **Given** the context menu is open, **When** the user reads the
72.    resolution items, **Then** the existing menu items (Open Config,
73.    Reload Config, Run at Startup, etc.) appear below the resolution
74.    details, separated by a divider.
75. 
76. ---
77. 
78. ### Edge Cases
79. 
80. - What happens when the scaling factor is a non-standard value
81.   (e.g., 125%, 175%)? The displayed percentage MUST match the
82.   actual system DPI scaling, rounded to the nearest whole percent.
83. - What happens when the primary display cannot be detected (e.g.,
84.   headless or remote desktop with no physical monitor)? The tooltip
85.   and context menu MUST show a graceful fallback message such as
86.   "Resolution: unavailable" rather than crashing or showing stale
87.   data.
88. - What happens when effective and native resolution are identical
89.   (100% scaling)? Both the tooltip and context menu MUST still show
90.   both W×H values and indicate 100% scaling for consistency.
91. - What happens with fractional scaling values like 137.5%? The
92.   display MUST round the percentage to the nearest whole number
93.   and both width and height to the nearest whole pixel.
94. - What happens when the tooltip text exceeds the 128-character
95.   system limit? The format MUST be concise enough to fit; if the
96.   profile name is long, it may be truncated with an ellipsis to
97.   keep resolution data intact.
98. 
99. ## Requirements *(mandatory)*
100. 
101. ### Functional Requirements
102. 
103. - **FR-001**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the current
104.   effective resolution as W×H in pixels.
105. - **FR-002**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the native
106.   (physical) resolution of the primary display as W×H in pixels.
107. - **FR-003**: The tray icon tooltip MUST display the current display
108.   scaling factor as a percentage (e.g., "125%", "200%").
109. - **FR-004**: The context menu MUST display the effective resolution
110.   (W×H), native resolution (W×H), and scaling percentage at the
111.   top of the menu, above all existing items.
112. - **FR-005**: The context menu resolution items MUST be separated
113.   from the rest of the menu by a visual divider.
114. - **FR-006**: All displayed resolution values MUST update
115.   automatically when the primary display changes (monitor switch,
116.   dock/undock, scaling change) within the existing poll interval.
117. - **FR-007**: The tooltip MUST remain within the operating system's
118.   tooltip character limit (currently 128 characters on Windows).
119. - **FR-008**: The tooltip and context menu MUST display whole-number
120.   pixel values and whole-number percentages (rounding to nearest).
121. 
122. ### Key Entities
123. 
124. - **Display Info**: Represents the current primary display state —
125.   native resolution (W×H physical pixels), effective resolution
126.   (W×H logical pixels after scaling), and scaling factor
127.   (percentage).
128. 
129. ## Success Criteria *(mandatory)*
130. 
131. ### Measurable Outcomes
132. 
133. - **SC-001**: Users can determine the effective resolution (W×H),
134.   native resolution (W×H), and scaling factor of their primary
135.   display by hovering over the tray icon, without opening any
136.   menu.
137. - **SC-002**: Users can see the same resolution breakdown at the
138.   top of the right-click context menu every time they open it.
139. - **SC-003**: The displayed resolution values match the values shown
140.   in Windows Display Settings for the primary monitor in 100% of
141.   tested scaling configurations (100%, 125%, 150%, 175%, 200%,
142.   225%, 250%, 300%).
143. - **SC-004**: Resolution information updates within one poll cycle
144.   (default 5 seconds) after a display change event (dock, undock,
145.   scaling change).
146. - **SC-005**: The tooltip text fits within the system tooltip
147.   character limit and is fully readable without truncation.
148. 
149. ## Assumptions
150. 
151. - The tooltip format will follow a pattern like:
152.   `"Ultrawide | 1920×1080 effective (3840×2160 @ 200%)"` —
153.   exact wording may be refined during implementation as long as
154.   all four data points (effective W×H, native W×H, scaling, and
155.   active profile) are present.
156. - The context menu will present the resolution info as read-only
157.   (disabled/greyed) menu items at the top, consistent with the
158.   existing status item style.
159. - The existing poll/event mechanism for detecting display changes
160.   is sufficient; no new detection mechanism is needed.
161. - Only the primary display's resolution is shown (multi-monitor
162.   display is out of scope for this feature).
163. - Height values are derived by applying the same scaling formula
164.   used for width (physicalHeight × 96 / dpiY).
165.
```

</details>

```
1. using Microsoft.Win32;
2. 
3. namespace TaskbarAlignmentTool;
4. 
5. /// <summary>
6. /// Monitors the primary display's effective resolution using Windows messages
7. /// (WM_DISPLAYCHANGE, WM_DPICHANGED) and a low-frequency fallback timer.
8. /// </summary>
9. internal sealed class DisplayMonitor : IDisposable
10. {
11.     private readonly System.Windows.Forms.Timer _fallbackTimer;
12.     private readonly DisplayChangeWindow _messageWindow;
13.     private int _lastWidth;
14.     private ResolutionMode _resolutionMode;
15. 
16.     public int EffectiveWidth => GetWidth(_resolutionMode);
17. 
18.     public event EventHandler<int>? ResolutionChanged;
19. 
20.     public DisplayMonitor(int fallbackIntervalMs = 60000, ResolutionMode resolutionMode = ResolutionMode.Effective)
21.     {
22.         _resolutionMode = resolutionMode;
23.         _lastWidth = EffectiveWidth;
24. 
25.         // Hidden window to receive WM_DISPLAYCHANGE and WM_DPICHANGED
26.         _messageWindow = new DisplayChangeWindow(OnDisplayMessage);
27. 
28.         // Also subscribe to the .NET event for display settings changes
29.         SystemEvents.DisplaySettingsChanged += OnDisplaySettingsChanged;
30. 
31.         // Low-frequency fallback timer (default 60s) as a safety net
32.         _fallbackTimer = new System.Windows.Forms.Timer
33.         {
34.             Interval = Math.Max(fallbackIntervalMs, 5000)
35.         };
36.         _fallbackTimer.Tick += OnFallbackTick;
37.         _fallbackTimer.Start();
38.     }
39. 
40.     /// <summary>Updates the fallback timer interval and resolution mode at runtime.</summary>
41.     public void UpdateInterval(int intervalMs, ResolutionMode? resolutionMode = null)
42.     {
43.         _fallbackTimer.Interval = Math.Max(intervalMs, 5000);
44.         if (resolutionMode.HasValue)
45.             _resolutionMode = resolutionMode.Value;
46.     }
47. 
48.     private void OnDisplayMessage()
49.     {
50.         CheckAndNotify();
51.     }
52. 
53.     private void OnDisplaySettingsChanged(object? sender, EventArgs e)
54.     {
55.         CheckAndNotify();
56.     }
57. 
58.     private void OnFallbackTick(object? sender, EventArgs e)
59.     {
60.         CheckAndNotify();
61.     }
62. 
63.     private void CheckAndNotify()
64.     {
65.         var current = EffectiveWidth;
66.         if (current != _lastWidth)
67.         {
68.             _lastWidth = current;
69.             ResolutionChanged?.Invoke(this, current);
70.         }
71.     }
72. 
73.     /// <summary>Forces a re-check and fires the event if the width changed (or always if force is true).</summary>
74.     public void Refresh(bool force = false)
75.     {
76.         var current = EffectiveWidth;
77.         if (force || current != _lastWidth)
78.         {
79.             _lastWidth = current;
80.             ResolutionChanged?.Invoke(this, current);
81.         }
82.     }
83. 
84.     private static int GetWidth(ResolutionMode mode)
85.     {
86.         var screen = Screen.PrimaryScreen;
87.         if (screen == null) return 0;
88. 
89.         int physicalWidth = screen.Bounds.Width;
90. 
91.         if (mode == ResolutionMode.Physical)
92.             return physicalWidth;
93. 
94.         try
95.         {
96.             var hMonitor = NativeMethods.MonitorFromPoint(0, NativeMethods.MONITOR_DEFAULTTOPRIMARY);
97.             if (hMonitor != nint.Zero &&
98.                 NativeMethods.GetDpiForMonitor(hMonitor, NativeMethods.MDT_EFFECTIVE_DPI, out uint dpiX, out _) == 0 &&
99.                 dpiX > 0)
100.             {
101.                 return (int)(physicalWidth * 96.0 / dpiX);
102.             }
103.         }
104.         catch
105.         {
106.             // Fall through to physical width
107.         }
108. 
109.         return physicalWidth;
110.     }
111. 
112.     public void Dispose()
113.     {
114.         SystemEvents.DisplaySettingsChanged -= OnDisplaySettingsChanged;
115.         _fallbackTimer.Stop();
116.         _fallbackTimer.Dispose();
117.         _messageWindow.DestroyHandle();
118.     }
119. 
120.     /// <summary>
121.     /// Hidden NativeWindow that receives WM_DISPLAYCHANGE and WM_DPICHANGED messages.
122.     /// </summary>
123.     private sealed class DisplayChangeWindow : NativeWindow
124.     {
125.         private readonly Action _onChange;
126. 
127.         public DisplayChangeWindow(Action onChange)
128.         {
129.             _onChange = onChange;
130.             var cp = new CreateParams
131.             {
132.                 Caption = "TaskbarAlignmentTool_DisplayMonitor",
133.                 // Message-only window (HWND_MESSAGE parent)
134.                 Parent = new nint(-3)
135.             };
136.             CreateHandle(cp);
137.         }
138. 
139.         protected override void WndProc(ref Message m)
140.         {
141.             if (m.Msg is NativeMethods.WM_DISPLAYCHANGE or NativeMethods.WM_DPICHANGED)
142.             {
143.                 _onChange();
144.             }
145.             base.WndProc(ref m);
146.         }
147.     }
148. }
149. 
```

```
1. using System.Diagnostics;
2. using Microsoft.Win32;
3. 
4. namespace TaskbarAlignmentTool;
5. 
6. /// <summary>
7. /// Application context that manages the system-tray icon, context menu,
8. /// and wires the display monitor to the taskbar aligner using resolution profiles.
9. /// </summary>
10. internal sealed class TrayApplicationContext : ApplicationContext
11. {
12.     private const string StartupRegistryPath = @"Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run";
13.     private const string StartupValueName = "TaskbarAlignmentTool";
14. 
15.     private AppConfig _config;
16.     private readonly DisplayMonitor _monitor;
17.     private readonly NotifyIcon _notifyIcon;
18.     private readonly ToolStripMenuItem _statusItem;
19.     private readonly ToolStripMenuItem _profileItem;
20.     private readonly ToolStripMenuItem _startupItem;
21. 
22.     private static readonly Lazy<bool> _isMsixPackaged = new(DetectMsixPackaged);
23. 
24.     private int _profileSwitchCount;
25. 
26.     public TrayApplicationContext(AppConfig config)
27.     {
28.         _config = config;
29.         _monitor = new DisplayMonitor(config.RefreshIntervalMs, config.ResolutionMode);
30. 
31.         _statusItem = new ToolStripMenuItem("Initializing...") { Enabled = false };
32.         _profileItem = new ToolStripMenuItem("Profile: —") { Enabled = false };
33.         _startupItem = new ToolStripMenuItem("Run at Startup", null, OnToggleStartup)
34.         {
35.             Checked = IsStartupEnabled()
36.         };
37. 
38.         var menu = new ContextMenuStrip();
39.         menu.Items.Add(_statusItem);
40.         menu.Items.Add(_profileItem);
41.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
42.         menu.Items.Add("Open Config", null, OnOpenConfig);
43.         menu.Items.Add("Reload Config", null, OnReloadConfig);
44.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
45.         menu.Items.Add(_startupItem);
46.         menu.Items.Add("Refresh Now", null, OnRefresh);
47.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
48.         menu.Items.Add(CreateDiagnosticsMenu());
49.         menu.Items.Add(new ToolStripSeparator());
50.         menu.Items.Add("Exit", null, OnExit);
51. 
52.         _notifyIcon = new NotifyIcon
53.         {
54.             Icon = RenderTrayIcon(0),
55.             ContextMenuStrip = menu,
56.             Text = "Taskbar Alignment Tool",
57.             Visible = true
58.         };
59. 
60.         _monitor.ResolutionChanged += OnResolutionChanged;
61. 
62.         // Apply profile immediately on startup
63.         ApplyForWidth(_monitor.EffectiveWidth);
64.     }
65. 
66.     private void OnResolutionChanged(object? sender, int effectiveWidth)
67.     {
68.         ApplyForWidth(effectiveWidth);
69.     }
70. 
71.     private void ApplyForWidth(int effectiveWidth)
72.     {
73.         var profile = _config.ResolveProfile(effectiveWidth);
74.         if (profile != null && TaskbarAligner.ApplyProfile(profile))
75.         {
76.             _profileSwitchCount++;
77.             if (_config.ShowNotifications)
78.             {
79.                 _notifyIcon.ShowBalloonTip(
80.                     _config.NotificationDurationMs,
81.                     "Taskbar Alignment Tool",
82.                     $"Switched to \"{profile.Name}\" ({effectiveWidth}px)",
83.                     ToolTipIcon.Info);
84.             }
85.         }
86.         UpdateStatus(effectiveWidth, profile);
87.     }
88. 
89.     private void UpdateStatus(int effectiveWidth, ProfileConfig? profile)
90.     {
91.         var profileName = profile?.Name ?? "None";
92.         _statusItem.Text = $"Width: {effectiveWidth}px";
93.         _profileItem.Text = $"Profile: {profileName}";
94.         _notifyIcon.Text = $"Taskbar: {profileName} ({effectiveWidth}px)";
95. 
96.         // Update tray icon with current width
97.         var oldIcon = _notifyIcon.Icon;
98.         _notifyIcon.Icon = RenderTrayIcon(effectiveWidth);
99.         oldIcon?.Dispose();
100.     }
101. 
102.     private void OnRefresh(object? sender, EventArgs e)
103.     {
104.         _monitor.Refresh(force: true);
105.     }
106. 
107.     private void OnOpenConfig(object? sender, EventArgs e)
108.     {
109.         var path = AppConfig.GetConfigPath();
110.         if (!File.Exists(path))
111.             _config.Save();
112.         Process.Start(new ProcessStartInfo(path) { UseShellExecute = true });
113.     }

115.     private void OnReloadConfig(object? sender, EventArgs e)
116.     {

118.         _monitor.UpdateInterval(_config.RefreshIntervalMs, _config.ResolutionMode);
119.         _monitor.Refresh(force: true);
120.     }

122.     private void OnToggleStartup(object? sender, EventArgs e)
123.     {
124.         if (IsStartupEnabled())
125.             DisableStartup();
126.         else
127.             EnableStartup();
128. 
129.         _startupItem.Checked = IsStartupEnabled();
130.     }
131. 
132.     private static bool IsMsixPackaged() => _isMsixPackaged.Value;
133. 
134.     private static bool DetectMsixPackaged()
135.     {
136.         try
137.         {
138.             // Windows.ApplicationModel.Package.Current throws if not packaged
139.             _ = Windows.ApplicationModel.Package.Current.Id;
140.             return true;
141.         }
142.         catch
143.         {
144.             return false;
145.         }
146.     }
147. 
148.     private static bool IsStartupEnabled()
149.     {
150.         if (IsMsixPackaged())
151.         {
152.             try
153.             {
154.                 var task = Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTask
155.                     .GetAsync("TaskbarAlignmentToolStartup").GetAwaiter().GetResult();
156.                 return task.State == Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTaskState.Enabled;
157.             }
158.             catch { return false; }
159.         }
160. 
161.         using var key = Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(StartupRegistryPath, false);
162.         return key?.GetValue(StartupValueName) != null;
163.     }
164. 
165.     private static void EnableStartup()
166.     {
167.         if (IsMsixPackaged())
168.         {
169.             try
170.             {
171.                 var task = Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTask
172.                     .GetAsync("TaskbarAlignmentToolStartup").GetAwaiter().GetResult();
173.                 task.RequestEnableAsync().GetAwaiter().GetResult();
174.             }
175.             catch { /* Startup task not available */ }
176.             return;
177.         }
178. 
179.         using var key = Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(StartupRegistryPath, true);
180.         var exePath = Application.ExecutablePath;
181.         key?.SetValue(StartupValueName, $"\"{exePath}\"");
182.     }
183. 
184.     private static void DisableStartup()
185.     {
186.         if (IsMsixPackaged())
187.         {
188.             try
189.             {
190.                 var task = Windows.ApplicationModel.StartupTask
191.                     .GetAsync("TaskbarAlignmentToolStartup").GetAwaiter().GetResult();
192.                 task.Disable();
193.             }
194.             catch { /* Startup task not available */ }
195.             return;
196.         }
197. 
198.         using var key = Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(StartupRegistryPath, true);
199.         key?.DeleteValue(StartupValueName, false);
200.     }
201. 
202.     private ToolStripMenuItem CreateDiagnosticsMenu()
203.     {
204.         var diag = new ToolStripMenuItem("Diagnostics");
205.         diag.DropDownOpening += (_, _) =>
206.         {
207.             diag.DropDownItems.Clear();
208.             var proc = System.Diagnostics.Process.GetCurrentProcess();
209.             var memMb = proc.WorkingSet64 / (1024.0 * 1024.0);
210.             var cpuTime = proc.TotalProcessorTime;
211.             diag.DropDownItems.Add(new ToolStripMenuItem($"Memory: {memMb:F1} MB") { Enabled = false });
212.             diag.DropDownItems.Add(new ToolStripMenuItem($"CPU time: {cpuTime.TotalSeconds:F2}s") { Enabled = false });
213.             diag.DropDownItems.Add(new ToolStripMenuItem($"Profile switches: {_profileSwitchCount}") { Enabled = false });
214.         };
215.         return diag;
216.     }
217. 
218.     private void OnExit(object? sender, EventArgs e)
219.     {
220.         _notifyIcon.Visible = false;
221.         _monitor.Dispose();
222.         _notifyIcon.Dispose();
223.         Application.Exit();
224.     }

226.     /// <summary>
227.     /// Renders a DPI-aware tray icon showing the current display width.
228.     /// Auto-detects Windows light/dark theme for contrast.
229.     /// </summary>
230.     private static Icon RenderTrayIcon(int width)
231.     {
232.         int dpi = GetSystemDpi();
233.         int size = (int)(16 * dpi / 96.0);
234.         float scale = size / 16f;
235.         bool isDarkTheme = IsSystemDarkTheme();
236. 
237.         using var bmp = new Bitmap(size, size);
238.         using var g = Graphics.FromImage(bmp);
239.         g.SmoothingMode = System.Drawing.Drawing2D.SmoothingMode.HighQuality;
240.         g.TextRenderingHint = System.Drawing.Text.TextRenderingHint.ClearTypeGridFit;
241.         g.PixelOffsetMode = System.Drawing.Drawing2D.PixelOffsetMode.HighQuality;
242.         g.Clear(Color.Transparent);
243. 
244.         // Background color adapts to theme
245.         var bgColor = isDarkTheme
246.             ? Color.FromArgb(255, 0, 150, 180)   // Teal on dark taskbar
247.             : Color.FromArgb(255, 0, 120, 150);  // Darker teal on light taskbar
248.         var textBrush = isDarkTheme ? Brushes.White : Brushes.White;
249. 
250.         using var bgBrush = new SolidBrush(bgColor);
251.         g.FillRectangle(bgBrush, 0, 0, size, size);
252. 
253.         // Display width as the full icon content
254.         var label = width.ToString();
255.         float fontSize = label.Length <= 3 ? 7f : label.Length == 4 ? 5.5f : 4.5f;
256.         using var font = new Font("Segoe UI", fontSize * scale, FontStyle.Bold, GraphicsUnit.Pixel);
257.         var sf = new StringFormat
258.         {
259.             Alignment = StringAlignment.Center,
260.             LineAlignment = StringAlignment.Center
261.         };
262.         var textRect = new RectangleF(0, 0, size, size);
263.         g.DrawString(label, font, textBrush, textRect, sf);
264. 
265.         return Icon.FromHandle(bmp.GetHicon());
266.     }
267. 
268.     /// <summary>
269.     /// Checks if Windows is using dark mode for apps.
270.     /// Registry: HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Themes\Personalize\SystemUsesLightTheme
271.     /// 0 = dark, 1 = light
272.     /// </summary>
273.     private static bool IsSystemDarkTheme()
274.     {
275.         try
276.         {
277.             using var key = Microsoft.Win32.Registry.CurrentUser.OpenSubKey(
278.                 @"Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Themes\Personalize", false);
279.             var value = key?.GetValue("SystemUsesLightTheme");
280.             if (value is int intVal)
281.                 return intVal == 0;
282.         }
283.         catch { }
284.         return true; // Default to dark
285.     }
286. 
287.     private static int GetSystemDpi()
288.     {
289.         try
290.         {
291.             var hMonitor = NativeMethods.MonitorFromPoint(0, NativeMethods.MONITOR_DEFAULTTOPRIMARY);
292.             if (hMonitor != nint.Zero &&
293.                 NativeMethods.GetDpiForMonitor(hMonitor, NativeMethods.MDT_EFFECTIVE_DPI, out uint dpiX, out _) == 0 &&
294.                 dpiX > 0)
295.             {
296.                 return (int)dpiX;
297.             }
298.         }
299.         catch { }
300.         return 96;
301.     }
302. }
303. 
```

## 4. **DisplayMonitor.cs — Height Tracking**

- Has `EffectiveWidth` property (line 16) and `_lastWidth` field

- Calculates effective width via DPI scaling:
```csharp
private static int GetWidth(ResolutionMode mode)
{
    var screen = Screen.PrimaryScreen;
    int physicalWidth = screen.Bounds.Width;
    if (mode == ResolutionMode.Physical)
        return physicalWidth;
    
    // Scales to effective DPI
    var hMonitor = NativeMethods.MonitorFromPoint(0, NativeMethods.MONITOR_DEFAULTTOPRIMARY);
    if (hMonitor != nint.Zero && 
        NativeMethods.GetDpiForMonitor(hMonitor, NativeMethods.MDT_EFFECTIVE_DPI, out uint dpiX, out _) == 0)
        return (int)(physicalWidth * 96.0 / dpiX);
}
```
